## **NATIONAL INSTITUTE FOR HEALTH AND CLINICAL EXCELLENCE** 

## **Health Technology Appraisal** 

**Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept for the treatment of rheumatoid arthritis after the failure of a TNF inhibitor (part review of NICE technology appraisal guidance 36, review of NICE technology appraisal guidance 126 and 141) Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the Appraisal Consultation Document (ACD)** 

## **Definitions:** 

**Consultees –** Organisations that accept an invitation to participate in the appraisal including the manufacturer or sponsor of the technology, national professional organisations, national patient organisations, the Department of Health and the Welsh Assembly Government and relevant NHS organisations in England. Consultee organisations are invited to submit evidence and/or statements and respond to consultations. They are also have right to appeal against the Final Appraisal Determination (FAD). Consultee organisations representing patients/carers and professionals can nominate clinical specialists and patient experts to present their personal views to the Appraisal Committee. 

**Clinical specialists and patient experts** – Nominated specialists/experts have the opportunity to make comments on the ACD separately from the organisations that nominated them. They do not have the right of appeal against the FAD other than through the nominating organisation. 

**Commentators –** Organisations that engage in the appraisal process but that are not asked to prepare an evidence submission or statement. They are invited to respond to consultations but, unlike consultees, they do not have the right of appeal against the FAD. These organisations include manufacturers of comparator technologies, NHS Quality Improvement Scotland, the relevant National Collaborating Centre (a group commissioned by the Institute to develop clinical guidelines), other related research groups where appropriate (for example, the Medical Research Council and National Cancer Research Institute); other groups (for example, the NHS Confederation, NHS Information Authority and NHS Purchasing and Supplies Agency, and the _British National Formulary_ ). 

**Public –** Members of the public have the opportunity to comment on the ACD when it is posted on the Institute’s web site 5 days after it is sent to consultees and commentators. These comments are usually presented to the appraisal committee in full, but may be summarised by the Institute secretariat – for example when many letters, emails and web site comments are received and recurring themes can be identified.

|Royal College of<br>Pathologists|The Royal College of Pathologists have no comments to make (on the<br>above ACD and evaluation report of the above appraisal) at this stage of the<br>development.|Comment noted. No actions required.|
|Royal College of<br>Nursing|**Has the relevant evidence been taken into account ?**<br>We recognise the challenges the Appraisal Committee has had to face in<br>gaining evidence to undertake a robust and realistic evaluation of the true<br>benefits (to the patient and the health economy) particularly when wider<br>social costs cannot be considered.  However, given this limitation, we feel<br>the scope has considered the evidence available.|Comment noted. No actions required.|
|Royal College of<br>Nursing|We note the Committee’s comments in Paragraph 2.5 – With respect to<br>missing data and the time frame taken for work disability to occur, there are<br>no data for socio economic costs, including patients who reduce working<br>hours / change work for sometimes lower paid employment. This can have a<br>significant effect on patient’s quality of life and their contribution to the wider<br>economy.|Comment noted. Section 2.5 is part of background<br>information to the condition and current<br>management. This section does not reflect the<br>considerations of the Committee. The Committee<br>recognised the impact of rheumatoid arthritis on<br>patients and its impact on employment (see FAD<br>section 4.3.2).|
|Royal College of<br>Nursing|Patient’s quality of life is also largely affected by other aspects of rheumatoid<br>arthritis such as pain, fatigue, and sleep disturbance which the report<br>recognises in paragraph 4.3.15 as not being incorporated in the HAQ score.<br>Failure to treat these aspects have the potential to affect a patient’s function,<br>and increase the individual’s use of primary care services, and clinical nurse<br>specialist facilities such as advice-lines and urgent appointments.|Comment noted. The Committee understood the<br>importance of pain, fatigue and sleep disturbance<br>with regards to their impact on quality of life. The<br>Committee was aware that such aspects may not<br>be captured by the HAQ score. The Committee<br>concluded that patients may derive benefits from<br>treatment that are not reflected in HAQ. (see FAD<br>section 4.3.17)|
|Royal College of<br>Nursing|We would agree with the paragraph 4.3.10 that treatment effects for<br>conventional DMARD’s after failure of Anti TNF therapy would be limited,<br>given that in order to meet the criteria for the use of TNF initially include<br>failure to respond to conventional DMARD therapy.|Comment noted. The Committee considered the<br>clinical effectiveness of conventional DMARDs and<br>concluded that their effect in people for whom a<br>TNF inhibitor had failed was likely to be small (see<br>FAD sections 4.3.12 and 4.3.23).|

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|

|Royal College of<br>Nursing|**Are the summaries of clinical and cost effectiveness reasonable**<br>**interpretations of the evidence, and are the preliminary views on the**<br>**resource impact and implications for the NHS appropriate?**<br>The Appraisal Committee themselves have highlighted mainly limitations to<br>the cost effectiveness and interpretations made.  We are concerned that<br>despite attempts by all involved to input into the economic model that we still<br>fail to capture the potential benefits to an individual rather than the group<br>effect.  Yet in reality we as clinicians are providing care to individuals who<br>may actually benefit significantly and have a strong individual need for an<br>effective treatment pathway.  As we are making significant decisions based<br>upon the group not the potential individual benefits of sequential use - we<br>are significantly compromising some individuals’ ability to benefit from<br>subsequent treatments e.g. sequential use of a TNF inhibitor following failure<br>of Rituximab.  Particularly as yet it is difficult to identify the most appropriate<br>pathway for an individual patient as research is not yet available to support<br>the use of one or another Anti TNF therapy as a first option.|Comment noted. The Committee understood that<br>rheumatoid arthritis is heterogeneous, that different<br>people can respond differently to the same<br>treatment. It also recognised that currently it is<br>difficult to predict whose disease will respond to a<br>given treatment (see FAD section 4.3.3). However,<br>the Committee is asked to make recommendations<br>for populations of individuals. Within this it may<br>consider subgroups for whom treatment may be<br>more clinically or cost effective.<br>For people who have contraindications to rituximab<br>or methotrexate or who require that rituximab<br>treatment be withdrawn because of an adverse<br>event, adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab and<br>abatacept are recommended as treatment options<br>(see FAD section 1). The Committee was not<br>presented with any evidence that enabled to it<br>make recommendations about the use of<br>adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab and abatacept<br>after the failure of rituximab (see FAD section<br>4.3.28).|
|sRoyal College of<br>Nursing|Where in the patient treatment pathway will Certolizumab pegol be placed?|Comment noted. Certolizumab pegol was subject to<br>its own single technology appraisal (see NICE<br>technology appraisal guidance 186). Certolizumab<br>pegol is recommended as an option for the<br>treatment of people with rheumatoid arthritis in the<br>same way as the other tumour necrosis factor<br>(TNF) inhibitor treatments in NICE technology<br>appraisal guidance 130 ‘Adalimumab, etanercept<br>and infliximab for the treatment of rheumatoid<br>arthritis’. The guidance for certolizumab pegol does<br>not include recommendations for sequential use<br>(seeFADsection 4.3.5).|

|Rheumatoid arthritis<br>Royal College of<br>Nursing|- Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee<br>**Point 4.3.20**The Appraisal Committee state in paragraph 4.3.20 that the<br>current guidance on stopping treatment is not fully implemented in clinical<br>practice, therefore, the response criterion had not been incorporated into the<br>BRAM model.  We are not clear what implication this has for the economic<br>model.  It does however highlight an issue that is likely to change as PCTs<br>robustly monitor biologics use as their knowledge and understanding of the<br>treatment and use of biologics improve and competencies of PCTs improve.<br>It is hoped that this modelling did not compromise the overall cost<br>effectiveness calculations to the detriment of the patient.  This also means<br>that the NHS resources are being more effectively used.  In addition, if there<br>are implications for the reality of delivering a range of treatment options to<br>the patient, the challenge clinicians experience on the ground is that much of<br>the time is taken up with paper work and negotiations with PCTs when<br>patients fall outside the current criteria.  It is likely that the greatest benefit<br>would be that patients who have failed to gain sufficient benefit can be<br>relatively easily identified.<br>We do not know whether the issues related exception reporting (requests for<br>patients to be funded/ endorsed by the PCT for further treatment when they<br>fall outside current NICE guidance recommendations) will be increased or<br>reduced applying the proposed recommendations.   The key issue is that the<br>patient should continue to have their disease controlled and the pathway<br>ensures that there are robust treatment criteria and clear rationale and<br>monitoring of those failing to receive benefit progressing to the next effective<br>treatment option.  Anecdotally we suggest that it is likely that patients would<br>prefer to progress to a further treatment at the cost of having ineffective<br>treatment stopped. Was this considered in patient reports for this ACD?<br>If patients on biologics fail one treatment the same patient population will be<br>moving onto a further treatment.  The Committee in line with evidence<br>suggest that 15% of RA patients will have aggressive disease – this is likely<br>to be the population who will continue to require biologic therapies with the<br>current high disease activity score criteria.   Is it the case that these cost<br>savings in relation to stopping ineffective treatment are then transferred to<br>benefit when patients transfers to an effective treatment?|, commentator and public comments on the ACD<br>~~Page 5 of 153~~<br> <br>Comment noted. The Committee heard from clinical<br>specialists that although implementing stopping<br>rules could be difficult, clinicians were increasingly<br>following guidance on stopping rules. The<br>Committee concluded that continuation rules should<br>be considered in the estimation of cost<br>effectiveness (see FAD section 4.3.22).<br>Comment noted. The Committee recognised the<br>impact of rheumatoid arthritis on patients (see FAD<br>section 4.3.2).The Committee must make<br>recommendations that take account of both the<br>clinical and cost effectiveness of an intervention<br>(Social Value Judgements – Principle for<br>development of NICE guidance, principle 3).<br>Comment noted. The economic model includes a<br>population of individuals for whom a first TNF<br>inhibitor has failed to control disease. The model<br>starts at the point at which the second biological<br>treatment is introduced. When a patient stops a<br>given treatment, the costs of that treatment<br>simultaneously stop. When a patient switches to a<br>new treatment, the benefits and costs of that new<br>treatment then start to be counted.|

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|Royal College of<br>Nursing|**Point 4.2.27:**We understand that costs for hospitalisation and joint<br>replacement were estimated using a cost per unit HAQ score but are unclear<br>where the rationale/evidence for this approach has been validated?|Comment noted. All the models submitted included<br>a cost of hospitalisation and joint replacement. The<br>sources of the data varied but included the BSRBR<br>and NOAR. The Assessment group included an<br>assumed cost per unit HAQ score rather than one<br>based on these data sources. However, this was<br>tested in sensitivity analyses where it was found<br>that the model was not very sensitive to this<br>parameter (see FAD section 4.3.15).|
|Royal College of<br>Nursing|We welcome the attempt to explore more fully the limitations of the HAQ<br>score and considering evidence in relation to the EQ5D.  A paper presented<br>at ACR in 2009 (Neovius et al) shows that there are significant heterogeneity<br>and that there are large subgroup differences that are likely to be important<br>when using the EQ5D.  They identify four distinct patient clusters first group<br>consisting of patients with low pre-treatment utility who experienced major<br>improvement, the second and third group consisting of patients with high or<br>low pre-treatment utility changed little on average with a small fourth group<br>with high utility as baseline deteriorated.<br>We note the comments by the Appraisal Committee that the results of using<br>HAQ and EQ-5D scores were subject to considerable uncertainty.  How did<br>this impact upon the modelling decisions?|Comment noted. The NICE reference case<br>specifies that economic models should use directly-<br>elicited health-related quality of life (HRQoL) data.<br>In addition, for adults there is a preference that this<br>is from EQ-5D (section 5.4.1 of the guide to the<br>methods of technology appraisal). None of the<br>economic models included directly elicited HRQoL<br>data using a generic measure (such as the EQ-5D)<br>and all relied on mapping in some way to get from a<br>disease specific measure (such as DAS score or<br>HAQ score) to a generic measure. The Committee<br>heard from the Assessment Group that the use of<br>alternate mapping functions did not significantly<br>change the estimated ICERs. The Committee<br>considered that mapping had shortcomings, but in<br>the absence of directly-elicited generic HRQoL<br>data, it was an acceptable way to derive estimates<br>of utility (see FAD section 4.3.20). The methods<br>guide states that the Committee will be more<br>cautious about recommending a technology when<br>they are less certain about the ICERs presented<br>(see methods guide section 6.2.23).|

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|Royal College of<br>Nursing|The National Audit Office Report (2009) reviewed the cost effectiveness of<br>biologic therapies in the context of wider implications and costs to the NHS.<br>They also produced an additional paper on health economics of their<br>findings (NAO 2009).  This evidence demonstrated that improved<br>management including biologic therapies were cost effective if the analysis<br>was considered over a five year period.    Has the Appraisal Committee<br>been aware of the modelling approach used by NAO and compared these<br>with the current approach with BRAM?|Comment noted. The model in the National Audit<br>Office (NAO) report focused on the treatment of<br>early rheumatoid arthritis, incorporating published<br>NICE guidance for TNF inhibitors to reflect<br>treatment for established disease. The current<br>appraisal starts at the point a TNF inhibitor has<br>failed. Therefore the NAO analysis and the current<br>appraisal respond to different questions. The time<br>horizon in this appraisal was life time and not 5<br>years, reflecting that the benefits and costs<br>(including cost savings) of treatment for rheumatoid<br>arthritis can accrue for the lifetime of the patient.|
|Royal College of<br>Nursing|We are unclear as to how important factors related to shortened life<br>expectancy and increased poor outcomes related to cardiovascular disease<br>have been considered in the model.  We presume a short life expectancy is<br>cost effective?   Patients may not die but face an additional health care<br>burden such as cardiovascular disease or osteoporosis with its potential risk<br>of fracture.|Comment noted. The economic models assume a<br>shorter life expectancy for people with rheumatoid<br>arthritis than for the general population. In addition,<br>the models include a cost of hospitalisation. These<br>factors are associated with HAQ score to the extent<br>that people with higher (worse) HAQ scores have a<br>greater number of associated costs and a shorter<br>life expectancy. Although fewer costs may be<br>accrued over a shortened life expectancy, so will<br>fewer quality-adjusted life years. As a result, it is not<br>the case that a shorter life expectancy will result in<br>cost effective treatment.|
|Royal College of<br>Nursing|We would welcome clarity about the changing patterns of RA management<br>as set out in the NICE RA management guidelines (2009) and how this<br>approach would have been considered in the model.  If as is hoped patients<br>will be eligible for treatment with biologic therapies much earlier in their<br>disease with less joint damage (however, they will as currently set out still<br>have to achieve a high level of disease activity at a DAS ≥5.1).|Comment noted. The Committee understood that<br>changes are occurring in the management of<br>rheumatoid arthritis The Committee was mindful of<br>this during its deliberations (see FAD section 4.3.4).<br>The appraisal considers a specific position in the<br>care pathway, that is after the failure of a TNF<br>inhibitor. Different patient populations can be<br>considered at this point in the pathway such as<br>people with lower disease activity. However,<br>consideration of subgroups is dependent on<br>availability and submission of data for the subgroup<br>and demonstration that the subgroup itself is robust.|

|Royal College of<br>Nursing|**Point 4.3.13:**Was the potential to avoid long term joint damage considered<br>in the sense of previous models and future models considered?|Comment noted. All the models submitted included<br>costs of hospitalisation and joint replacement (see<br>FAD sections 4.2.5, 4.2.9, 4.2.13, 4.2.17, 4.2.21,<br>4.2.25). People with<br>higher (worse) HAQ scores were modelled as<br>having greater associated costs of hospitalisation<br>and joint replacement. Would pressure to ensure treatment is stopped when<br>ineffective be a good approach with a greater option for new therapies being<br>offered?|Comment noted. The Committee heard from clinical<br>specialists that although implementing stopping<br>rules could be difficult, clinicians were increasingly<br>following guidance on stopping rules. The<br>Committee concluded that continuation rules should<br>be considered in the estimation of cost<br>effectiveness (see FAD section 4.3.22).|

|Royal College of<br>Nursing|The additional workload for nurses will be as a result of spending more time<br>with highly complex patients who have no effective treatment option, there<br>will be psychological support particularly with respect to withdrawal of<br>treatments, additional support for flare and poor disease control.  The impact<br>of this is likely to be an increase in the use of telephone advice line for<br>support and liaison and an increase in the use of inpatient facilities for<br>urgent access for inpatient beds (e.g. for intravenous methylprednisolone<br>infusions).  The long term consequences (>5 years) will be difficult to<br>quantify depending upon future decisions but potentially a small group of<br>patients will require high level nursing support related to symptom<br>management, increased co-morbidities and surgery.  For example,  multiple<br>joint replacements, fusion of the neck to resolve instability due to erosion of<br>odontoid peg, tissue viability issues such as managing patients requiring<br>long term treatment for vasculitis and leg ulcers, pinch grafts and<br>cardiovascular /osteoporosis management and associated fractures. This<br>may be translated in the future into increased community nursing support<br>and use of day care and or nursing home facilities.<br>The ongoing audit and data collection together with completion of specific<br>reports to PCTs remain an important but additional workload for nurses.|Comment noted. The models submitted by Bristol-<br>Myers Squibb and by Roche included costs of<br>palliative care. The Assessment Group’s model also<br>included the costs of palliative care. This cost<br>estimate was subject to sensitivity analyses where it<br>was identified that the estimates of cost<br>effectiveness were not very sensitive to this factor<br>(see FAD sections 4.2.5, 4.2.9, 4.2.13, 4.2.17,<br>4.2.21, 4.2.25, 4.3.15).|

|---|---|---|
|Royal College of<br>Nursing|**Are the provisional recommendations of the Appraisal Committee**<br>**sound and do they constitute a suitable basis for the preparation of**<br>**guidance top the NHS?**<br>We recognise the challenges in undertaking such complex modelling.<br>However, we still perceive the evidence and the crafting within the models<br>weighs rather heavily on the cost effectiveness components that are easily<br>measurable and fails to balance these within the model of the wider<br>healthcare and societal costs that we recognise remain a challenge with the<br>NHS.  Despite the challenges, we still feel it is important that these are given<br>fair and detailed consideration before the final determination is made.   We<br>hope the modelling considerations in the NAO report and the focus on<br>extending the model to a 5 year approach would be helpful.|Comment noted. As per the NICE reference case<br>(see sections 5.2.7 to 5.2.10 of the NICE methods<br>guide), costs incurred outside the NHS or PSS<br>were not incorporated.<br>The model in the National Audit Office (NAO) report<br>focused on the treatment of early rheumatoid<br>arthritis, incorporating published NICE guidance for<br>TNF inhibitors to reflect treatment for established<br>disease. The current appraisal starts at the point a<br>TNF inhibitor has failed. Therefore the NAO<br>analysis and the current appraisal respond to<br>different questions. The time horizon in this<br>appraisal was life time and not 5 years, reflecting<br>that the benefits and costs (including cost savings)<br>of treatment for rheumatoid arthritis can accrue for<br>the lifetime of the patient.|
|Royal College of<br>Nursing|**Are there any equality related issues that need special consideration**<br>**that are not covered in the ACD?**<br>The HAQ and the ACR 20, 50, 70 criteria are tools used to measure the<br>group response and have not been used to evaluate within those groups the<br>numbers of people who would have had an individual and significant benefit<br>to treatment.   This has true significance when social and wider health care<br>perspectives fail to be adequately considered.  Some patients will be<br>affected by this ACD more than others but there are no specific issues<br>otherwise to be considered.|Comment noted. The Committee makes<br>recommendations for the population of people<br>identified in the scope. Within this, it may consider<br>subgroups of people for whom treatment may be<br>more clinically or cost effective. When considering<br>subgroups, these should be clearly defined and<br>preferably be identified on the basis of a priori<br>expectation of known differential clinical or cost<br>effectiveness due to known biologically plausible<br>mechanisms, social characteristics or other clearly<br>justified factors (methods guide section 5.10.1). The<br>Committee has considered the subgroups identified<br>by consultees and included in the scope. The<br>subgroups identified were based on reason for<br>previous withdrawal of treatment and test of<br>seronegativity (FAD section 4.3.10, 4.3.11).|

|British Society for<br>Rheumatology|**Over estimation of response to DMARDS after TNF failure**<br>In 4.3.10 the committee concluded;_“That, on the basis of clinical opinion, the_<br>_effect of conventional DMARDs in people for whom a TNF inhibitor had failed was_<br>_likely to be small, but the relative effect in comparison with biological treatments was_<br>_not currently quantifiable”._<br>In the Addendum Report, from the West Midlands Health Technology<br>Assessment Collaboration the assessment group concluded on p77 that;<br>“t_he results were fairly sensitive to the assumptions on efficacy of conventional_<br>_DMARDs given after biologic therapy. The differences between the reference case_<br>_results in the BRAM and those produced by Abbott and Schering-Plough can be_<br>_explained by changing a small number of parameters in the model.”_<br>We broadly agree with these conclusions. As we discussed at the Appraisal<br>Committee meeting, we would particularly support the poor late DMARDs<br>scenario, as there is evidence to support poor benefits from conventional<br>DMARDs after the failure of anti-TNF. Analysis of the BeSt trial suggested<br>that if patients fail on methotrexate in any of the conventional treatment<br>arms, there is only a 15% chance that they will respond to subsequent<br>conventional DMARDs (van der Kooij SM et al. Ann Rheum Dis<br>2007;66:1356-62). Furthermore, this was in patients not exposed to anti-<br>TNF, which would suggest that in patients failing on anti-TNF, the success<br>rate on subsequent DMARDs would be even lower. We wish to emphasise<br>that the expected response in patients with established RA is anticipated to<br>be even worse than that seen in the BeSt study.  We feel that an estimate of<br>0% improvement on conventional DMARDs after the failure of anti-TNF is<br>likely to be much closer to reality than the 50% improvement quoted in<br>previous BRAM models. Table 21 on page 76 of the Addendum Report<br>shows, under a variety of different scenarios, changing from adalimumab to<br>infliximab achieves ICERs close to £20,000. We would suggest that Table<br>21 supports the cost effectiveness of infliximab following the failure of<br>adalimumab, and that this should be an alternative strategy to rituximab. We<br>would like to seek clarification on why other approaches such as IFX-ETN<br>and IFX-ADA are not included in the Table?<br>We therefore broadly agree with the committee’s conclusions in 4.3.16;_“that_<br>_the Assessment Group may have overestimated the efficacy of conventional_<br>_DMARDs”,_and urge the committee to consider the ICERs described in the<br>addendum report by the assessment group_._|Comment noted. The Committee considered that<br>the assumed 50% reduction in efficacy of<br>conventional DMARDs to be an underestimate of<br>the reduction in efficacy, when conventional<br>DMARDs are used in established as opposed to<br>early disease. However, it did not accept that there<br>would be no effect at all associated with therapy<br>(see FAD sections 4.3.12 and 4.3.23). The<br>Committee considered the ICERs reflecting a<br>greater reduction in conventional DMARD efficacy<br>in their deliberations.<br>Please note that the Addendum Report is<br>considered a product solely of the Assessment<br>Group. As a result, NICE cannot comment on what<br>tables included within that report do or do not<br>include. The column referred to in Table 21 does<br>not compare changing from adalimumab to<br>infliximab. This column shows the ICERs for the<br>comparison of infliximab and adalimumab. The<br>ICERs in table 21 have to be considered alongside<br>those in comparison with conventional DMARDs<br>and also those in comparison with rituximab.|



Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD Page 12 of 153 



<!-- page 13 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|British Society for<br>Rheumatology|**Inappropriate use of HAQ multiplier**<br>We would also wish to agree with the conclusion in 4.3.15; “t_he Committee_<br>_concluded that patients may derive benefits from the treatment that are not_<br>_reflected in HAQ score because of irreversible joint damage”._<br>However, we wish to express concern that this has not been taken into<br>account in the assessment report.Aletaha et al (Arthritis & Rheum 2006; 54:<br>2784-2792) were able to quantify the reduced response of the HAQ to<br>treatment in established disease. They found that among the 295 patients in<br>whom clinical remission was achieved, the average HAQ scores despite<br>clinical remission increased progressively with the duration of RA, from 0.19<br>(<2 years of RA) to 0.36 (2-<5 years) to 0.38 (5-<10 years) to 0.55 (<br>10<br>years) (_P_< 0.001). In addition they found that the reversibility of HAQ scores<br>decreased with the duration of RA (median 100%, 83.3%, 81.9%, and<br>66.7%, respectively;_P_< 0.001). We consider that these observations should<br>have been taken into account with the assessment group modelling and<br>would identify a greater improvement in utility from treatment.<br>We also consider that this data suggests the use of the HAQ multiplier to be<br>inappropriate.The Committee considered in 4.3.16; “_that the use of such a_<br>_multiplier to model changes in HAQ meant that absolute changes in the_<br>_upper range of the HAQ scores were larger than those in the lower range,_<br>_and that therefore people with more severe disease would have larger HAQ_<br>_improvements than if the HAQ scores from the clinical studies were used_<br>_directly. Bearing in mind these considerations, the Committee accepted the_<br>_use of a HAQ multiplier as a reasonable way to model changes in HAQ_<br>_score”._<br>This approach would be relevant in patients without irreversible disability but<br>is likely to underestimate the benefits of treatment in patients with late<br>disease who have established joint damage and would hope the<br>assessment group would be able to model the health economic analysis to<br>take these data into account.|Comment noted. As indicated in section 4.3.15 of<br>the ACD (section 4.3.17 of the FAD), the<br>Committee was mindful of the limitations of HAQ<br>score, including that it may be subject to ‘ceiling<br>effects’, and that it does not incorporate symptoms<br>such as pain, fatigue and sleep disturbance. The<br>Committee bore these limitations in mind during it<br>deliberations.<br>Similarly, the Committee considered the limitations<br>of the HAQ multiplier in its deliberations (see FAD<br>section 4.3.20).The use of a multiplier to represent<br>improvement (reduction) in HAQ score owing to<br>treatment gives a greater reduction to higher<br>(worse) HAQ scores. As a result, a multiplier may<br>over estimate the benefits of treatment in patients<br>with established rheumatoid arthritis who tend to<br>have higher HAQ scores because where there is<br>irreversible damage, it would be expected that the<br>benefits of treatment would be smaller.|



Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 14 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|British Society for<br>Rheumatology|**Failure to incorporate stopping rules**<br>We are concerned that the health economic analysis by the assessment<br>group does not take into account stopping rules as expressed in the NICE<br>guidance and BSR guidelines. In 4.3.20 it is stated that; “the Committee<br>heard from the clinical specialists that data from the British Society for<br>Rheumatology Biologics Register indicate that a number of people will<br>continue treatment with a TNF inhibitor even in the absence of such a<br>response, indicating that the use of stopping rules does not reflect current<br>clinical practice. It further heard from the Assessment Group that for this<br>reason stopping rules based on a response criterion had not been<br>incorporated into the Birmingham Rheumatoid Arthritis Model base-case<br>analysis. The Committee understood that the Birmingham Rheumatoid<br>Arthritis Model was not designed in a way which could incorporate stopping<br>rules based on a response criterion. The Committee noted, however, that a<br>scenario analysis which included the proportions of people stopping<br>treatment early that were used in the manufacturers’ response-based<br>models lowered the ICERs for the TNF inhibitors and abatacept by<br>approximately £10,000 per QALY gained. The Committee did not consider<br>that the Assessment Group’s analysis could be used as a basis for decision<br>making because it did not fully incorporate response criteria. In addition, the<br>Committee questioned if the application of such response criteria would be<br>reflective of clinical practice”.<br>It is our view that health economic evaluation must include stopping rules as<br>this is adopted by responsible prescribers and that NICE guidance should be<br>based on best treatment and clinical excellence and not a pragmatic<br>approach by some rheumatologists. In addition we are aware that health<br>commissioners are increasingly likely to ‘police’ the stopping rules of<br>patients. We consider that it is inappropriate not to incorporate stopping<br>rules in the analysis while issuing guidance that patients should stop<br>treatment if there is inadequate response.|Comment noted. The Committee heard from clinical<br>specialists that although implementing stopping<br>rules could be difficult, clinicians were increasingly<br>following guidance on stopping rules. The<br>Committee concluded that continuation rules should<br>be considered in the estimation of cost<br>effectiveness (see FAD section 4.3.22).|

Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 19 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|Wyeth (Pfizer)|Results from the SUNRISE trial cast doubt on the assumption that initial<br>HAQ improvements are maintained over the long term with repeat dosing of<br>rituximab. The mean reduction in HAQ score at 48 weeks, despite re-dosing<br>after 24 weeks was 0.27 (18% reduction in baseline HAQ of 1.5), compared<br>with the 0.40 reduction at 24 weeks observed in the REFLEX study.<br>Wyeth’s assertion that HAQ improvements with etanercept remain constant<br>on treatment is based on direct RCT observation coupled with evidence that<br>etanercept halts radiographic progression of disease for at least 3 years.<br>Whilst rituximab significantly lowers the rate of joint damage in a similar<br>patient population, the failure to halt progression together with the lack of<br>long-term HAQ data brings into question the base-case assumption that<br>HAQ score remains constant irrespective of the biological DMARD used.<br>The recent publication of the SUNRISE trial, sponsored by the manufacturer,<br>noted that retreatment with rituximab typically occurred 30 to 40 weeks<br>apart, depending on the trial. This resulted in worsening disease activity on<br>average between retreatment courses. Worsening of most components of<br>the ACR response criteria were first observed 28 – 32 weeks after initial<br>dosing. The paper concludes ‘Because the goals of retreatment include<br>maintenance of efficacy and prevention of flare, retreatment should occur<br>prior to worsening, and therefore Week 24 appeared to be an appropriate<br>time to retreat in most patients’.<br>Thus the Assessment Group’s reference case analysis, which assumes<br>repeat dosing of rituximab every 8.7 months (38 weeks), both over estimates<br>the efficacy and underestimates the costs associated with rituximab<br>treatment. It would therefore be more appropriate for the Appraisal<br>Committee to consider the scenario analysis which assumed a time to<br>retreatment of 6 months to be the most plausible estimate of the incremental<br>cost effectiveness of rituximab at £32,600 per QALY gained.<br>Whilst we acknowledge that the SUNRISE data was not available to the<br>Appraisal Committee at the time they developed the ACD, given the large<br>contribution the study makes to the evidence base for rituximab treatment,<br>incorporation of the findings from this study into the FAD would ensure the<br>robustness of the final guidance.|Comment noted. The Committee discussed the<br>results of the SUNRISE trial (see FAD section<br>4.1.7). It concluded that while 8.7 months between<br>treatments may be an over estimate of the re-<br>treatment interval, it would not be the case that<br>every person required rituximab re-treatment every<br>6 months (see FAD section 4.3.21).|

The<br>Committee recognised that data suggested that the<br>absolute response rates for rituximab were lower for<br>this group than for those who were seropositive.<br>However, it considered that the clinical data did still<br>suggest a benefit of treatment with rituximab. This<br>was supported by clinical opinion. On balance, the<br>Committee was not persuaded that the evidence<br>supported differential recommendations for this<br>subgroup (see FAD section 4.3.11).|

The BRAM shows that abatacept produces more QALYs in comparison<br>to rituximab, therefore it can be assumed that abatacept is more effective<br>than rituximab (12).|Comment noted. A variety of data were submitted<br>from each of the manufacturers about the<br>progression of disease while on the different<br>biological treatments. The Committee was not<br>persuaded that the evidence was sufficient to<br>support an assumption that abatacept differentially<br>altered progression of disease more so than the<br>other biological treatments. The Committee agreed<br>to consider the estimates of cost effectiveness<br>which assumed no deterioration in HAQ while on<br>treatment with a biological treatment. (See FAD<br>section 4.3.19).<br>The Committee makes recommendations to the<br>NHS based on both clinical and cost effectiveness.<br>To that end, the Committee takes into account both<br>QALYs and costs in its deliberations.|

Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|

|Arthritis Care|**Arthritis and Arthritis Care**<br>1.<br>Arthritis is the biggest cause of physical disability in the UK, affecting<br>up to 10 million people, including 12,000 children, and accounting for 30% of<br>GP visits. It carries a huge economic as well as human and social cost,<br>estimated at £7 billion annually in terms of lost labour in 2007.<br>2.<br>Arthritis Care is the UK’s leading organisation working with and for<br>people with all forms of arthritis. We offer people with arthritis the information<br>and support they need to make informed choices about managing their<br>arthritis, to reach their potential in society and to fully participate in their<br>communities.<br>3.<br>We believe that people with arthritis are entitled to receive the best<br>available treatment and medication, and to have their voice heard in<br>decisions affecting their health–as enshrined in the NHS Constitution.|Comments noted. No actions required.|



Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 43 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|Arthritis Care|**Rheumatoid arthritis**<br>Rheumatoid arthritis (RA) is a lifelong, progressive, musculoskeletal<br>condition that causes severe pain, swelling and inflammation of the joints,<br>and can lead to reduced joint function and disability. Approximately 10% of<br>people with RA have the condition in a particularly severe form, manifesting<br>itself as relentless pain and swelling, often in multiple joints. This causes<br>severe disability and loss of function, meaning that simple daily tasks,<br>including self-care, can become impossible without assistance.<br>Severe RA is extremely serious. 30% of people with untreated severe RA<br>will die within 5 years, a figure comparable with triple vessel Coronary Heart<br>Disease or stage III Hodgkin’s Disease. While someone with RA can expect<br>to live 5 years less than someone without it on average, much of this is<br>accounted for by the massively reduced life expectancy of the population<br>with severe RA.<br>A recent report by the National Audit Office (NAO) on services for people<br>with RA revealed that the number of people with RA is much higher than<br>previously thought, estimated at 580,000 people in England alone, with<br>26,000 new cases diagnosed each year. It also found that RA has annual<br>healthcare costs of £560 million to the NHS, with costs to the economy of<br>£1.8 billion in sick leave and work-related disability.|Comments noted. The Committee was aware of the<br>impact of rheumatoid arthritis on quality of life and<br>employment (see FAD section 4.3.2)|



Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 44 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|Arthritis Care|The NAO report clearly emphasised the importance of aggressively treating<br>RA within three months from the onset of symptoms, as this can stop the<br>development of the condition in its tracks and lead to remission. After the<br>first three months, the impact of any treatment or medication is vastly<br>reduced.<br>The report also found an enormous variation in spending across PCTs in<br>England, amounting to a postcode lottery. Those that do receive treatment<br>for RA often do not receive sufficiently high-quality treatment.<br>The Public Accounts Committee released a report in February this year<br>reaffirming the findings and endorsing the recommendations of the NAO<br>report.<br>What these findings demonstrate is that very large numbers of people are<br>living in often severe and debilitating pain because they are not getting the<br>services and the treatment they need. Central to this is prompt access to the<br>best available medication, including anti-TNFs.<br>NICE’s position regarding the availability of anti-TNF medication and the<br>ability of clinicians to prescribe more than one particular anti-TNF for<br>sequential treatment, based on the patient’s responsiveness to it, should<br>therefore be viewed with this context, and these findings, firmly in mind.|Comments noted. The Committee recognised the<br>importance placed on the availability of a variety of<br>medications (see FAD section 4.3.2).|
|Arthritis Care|Arthritis Care is extremely disappointed with the preliminary findings of this<br>consultation, which do not reflect the majority of medical opinion on anti-TNF<br>treatment for RA and which do not seem to take any account of either the<br>real experience or indeed the wishes of people with RA, whom these<br>treatments are intended to serve.<br>The preliminary findings appear to entirely ignore the patient dimension of<br>RA, and sit decidedly at odds with the growing consensus on the importance<br>of a more patient-centred health service, patient involvement in decisions<br>affecting their health and patient choice – all of which are enshrined in<br>numerous and varied high-profile documents, from the NHS Constitution to<br>High Quality Care for All to the World Class Commissioning Framework.|Comment noted. The Committee consider all of the<br>evidence submitted, which includes statements<br>from clinical specialists and patient experts (see<br>FAD sections 4.3.2 – 4.3.4). For both legal and<br>bioethical reasons, those undertaking technology<br>appraisals must take account of economic<br>considerations (see Social Value Judgements –<br>Principles for the development of NICE guidance;<br>principle 5).|



Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 45 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|Arthritis Care|NICE’s own guideline on the management of RA in adults, issued in<br>February 2009, emphasises the importance of person-centred care:<br>“Treatment and care should take into account peoples’ needs and<br>preferences. People with RA should have the opportunity to make informed<br>decisions about their care and treatment, in partnership with their healthcare<br>professionals.” (p.6)<br>The preliminary findings, however, appear to negate this, as they unduly<br>restrict the options available to both clinicians and people with RA, and<br>therefore the real choice available to people with RA with respect to their<br>health needs.|Comment noted. The Committee recognised the<br>importance placed on the availability of a variety of<br>medications. The Committee heard from clinical<br>specialists that the pathway of care following the<br>failure of treatment with a TNF inhibitor depends on<br>the individual persons’ responses to therapies, the<br>clinical experience of the physician and the<br>person’s preference (see FAD section 4.3.2).<br>Although NICE accepts that individual NHS users<br>will expect to receive treatments to which their<br>conditions may respond, this does not impose a<br>requirement on the Committee to recommend<br>technologies that are not cost effective enough to<br>provide the best value to users of the NHS as a<br>whole (see ‘Social Value Judgement – Principles for<br>the development of NICE guidance; principle 5).<br>Please see section 1 for the final recommendations.|
|Arthritis Care|Far from helping to provide a more efficient or better quality service to the<br>over half a million people with RA in England, these findings, if implemented,<br>will impact severely on the health and quality of life of many thousands of<br>people, and the overall effect will be entirely counterproductive in terms of<br>the long-term or indeed short-termgains,economic or otherwise.|Comment noted. For both legal and bioethical<br>reasons, those undertaking technology appraisals<br>must take account of economic considerations (see<br>Social Value Judgements – Principles for the<br>development of NICE guidance; principle 5).|
|Arthritis Care|There is abundant evidence, including a very large number of firsthand<br>testimonies from clinicians and people with RA, who are best placed to know<br>how any specific treatment is or is not helping them, which demonstrates<br>that different anti-TNFs work differently for different people, and it is only by<br>being able to try different treatments that many people are able to find the<br>one that actually works for them. This for them is not a whim; it is a need.|Comment noted. The Committee considered all of<br>the evidence submitted, which included statements<br>from clinical specialists and patient experts. The<br>Committee recognised that different people may<br>respond differently to any given treatment (see FAD<br>sections 4.3.2 – 4.3.4).|



Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 46 -->

## Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|Arthritis Care|Additionally, there is no clinical, anecdotal or practical evidence to support<br>the decision to allow the use of rituximab in combination with methotrexate<br>but not anti-TNFs for sequential use. Each anti-TNF is different and will work<br>for some people but not others. For many, rituximab simply does not work.|Comment noted. The directions from the Secretary<br>of State for Health requests the Institute to make<br>recommendations to the NHS based on both clinical<br>and cost effectiveness. The appraisal considers the<br>cost effectiveness of a treatment which incorporates<br>both the costs and benefits. For people who have<br>contraindications to rituximab or methotrexate or<br>who require that rituximab treatment be withdrawn<br>because of an adverse event, adalimumab,<br>etanercept, infliximab and abatacept are<br>recommended as treatment options (see FAD<br>section 1, see methods guide section 6.1.3). The<br>Committee was not presented with any evidence<br>that enabled to it make recommendations about the<br>use of adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab and<br>abatacept after the failure of rituximab (see FAD<br>section 4.3.28).|
|Arthritis Care|Lord Darzi made it clear that quality is the unifying principle behind the NHS.<br>Surely, therefore, any decision on the availability and sequential use of anti-<br>TNFs must be taken with the best interests of the population at heart, and<br>should only consider cost issues in this light, i.e. where they do not impact<br>negatively on the overall quality of service for the people the NHS is there to<br>serve.|Comment noted. The directions from the Secretary<br>of State for Health requests the Institute to make<br>recommendations to the NHS based on both clinical<br>and cost effectiveness. The appraisal considers the<br>cost effectiveness of a treatment which incorporates<br>both the costs and benefits (see Guide to the<br>methods of technology appraisal, section 6.1.3).|
|Arthritis Care|Arthritis Care therefore urges NICE to review its preliminary findings, taking<br>account of the clinical evidence which exists on the real use and impact of<br>anti-TNF treatment, and which clearly demonstrates the importance of a<br>wide range of options for sequential anti-TNF treatment. We also urge NICE<br>to consider this evidence in light of the fundamental importance of person-<br>centred care and of ensuring the best possible outcomes for people with RA,<br>based on their needs and their wishes.|Comment noted. The final recommendations for<br>adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab and abatacept<br>differ from the preliminary recommendations. For<br>people who are contraindicated to rituximab or<br>methotrexate or who require rituximab treatment be<br>withdrawn because of an adverse event,<br>adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab and abatacept<br>are recommended as treatment options (see FAD<br>section 1).|



Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 47 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|Arthritis Care|**Background to the current consultation**<br>The current NICE consultation on anti-TNF treatments is the latest in a long<br>line of deliberations on the issue of anti-TNFs. Most recently, NICE<br>announced its intention to restrict the sequential use of anti-TNFs 2008,  in<br>much the same way as at present.<br>At the time, the Arthritis and Musculoskeletal Alliance (ARMA), also<br>speaking on behalf of Arthritis Care, described NICE’s proposal as a<br>“prescription for pain,” on the grounds that it withdrew available treatment<br>options and condemned many people with RA to a life of debilitating pain.<br>ARMA also made a detailed submission to Dr. Carole Longson, Director of<br>the Centre for Health Technology Evaluation, regarding NICE’s proposals,<br>the substance of which is still equally valid in relation to the current<br>preliminary findings. We are attaching a copy of this submission, for your<br>reference and information.|Comments noted. The Committee has considered<br>all the evidence submitted. The Committee was<br>aware of the impact of rheumatoid arthritis on<br>quality of life, employment and the importance<br>placed on the availability of a variety of medications<br>(see FAD sections 4.3.2 – 4.3.3).|
|Arthritis Care|**The importance of sequential use of anti-TNFs**<br>Between 20,000 and 40,000 people in England and Wales are taking an<br>anti-TNF at any one time, and 50% have needed to switch treatments at<br>least once.<br>In order for people with RA to receive the treatment that actually works for<br>them, and clinicians need access to the widest possible range of treatments<br>in order to provide the best possible care for patients. The British Society for<br>Rheumatology Biologics Register shows that 70% of patients who switch<br>anti-TNFs derive a benefit from the second one, and this has been<br>established good practice in the UK for some years. These therapies are<br>already available for clinicians to use sequentially across Europe, and it is<br>perverse that they should not be available in the UK.|Comment noted. The Committee considered<br>evidence from the British Society for Rheumatology<br>Biologics Register (see FAD section 4.3.8).<br>With regards to the access to these technologies<br>across Europe, funding decisions for drugs are<br>each country’s individual responsibility. Funding<br>decisions can differ across countries because of the<br>different criteria applied.|



Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 48 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|Arthritis Care|Clinicians themselves stress the importance of being able to try different<br>anti-TNF treatments for individual patients. Professor Rob Moots, a clinician<br>and Professor of Rheumatology at the University of Liverpool, for example,<br>has said that “it’s almost impossible to know which anti-TNF will work for a<br>patient at the outset.” He has described NICE proposals to restrict the<br>options for anti-TNF treatment available to clinicians, as “flying in the face of<br>clinical judgement”, stating that “many patients will be left in astonishing<br>pain”, while clinicians will be left knowing that they haven’t explored all the<br>options for them.|Comment noted. The Committee considered all of<br>the evidence submitted, which included statements<br>from clinical specialists and patient experts. The<br>Committee recognised that currently there are<br>difficulties in targeting treatments to people most<br>likely to respond (see FAD sections 4.3.2 – 4.3.4).|
|Arthritis Care|The importance of this is illustrated very clearly and very powerfully by the<br>firsthand testimonies of people with RA themselves, many of whom have<br>had to try a number of different anti-TNFs before they could find one that<br>worked for them, and many of whom have yet to find the one that does<br>because they have been unable to try more treatments so far. In some<br>cases, certain anti-TNFs have worked initially but then ceased to work, and<br>in other cases certain anti-TNFs which did not work originally seemed to<br>work better only after the person had gone on to try another. In almost all<br>cases, however, the difference which finding the right anti-TNF treatment<br>has made to that person’s life has been transformational. Very often, this<br>has made the difference between having a good-quality life and being able<br>to live independently and remain in or return to work, and living in chronic,<br>debilitating pain and being reliant on others and the health sector for basic<br>needs.|Comment noted. The Committee considered all of<br>the evidence submitted, which included statements<br>from clinical specialists and patient experts about<br>the impact of rheumatoid arthritis and its<br>management (see FAD sections 4.3.2 – 4.3.4). The<br>Committee considered the subgroups of people<br>whose disease may not have responded to<br>treatment with a TNF inhibitor, and the group for<br>whom response may reduce over time (See FAD<br>section 4.3.10). The Institute must take into account<br>both the clinical and cost effectiveness of treatment<br>when making recommendations.|
|Arthritis Care|Arthritis Care has collated a number of personal testimonies from people<br>with RA in the attached Appendix. We urge NICE to read these testimonies<br>to gain an accurate picture of the real experiences – and the real needs - of<br>people with RA, and what this means for the regulation of anti-TNF<br>treatment.<br>**Appendix received but not reproduced here.**|Comments noted. The Committee has considered<br>all the evidence submitted. The Committee was<br>aware of the impact of rheumatoid arthritis on<br>quality of life, employment and the importance<br>placed on the availability of a variety of<br>medications. This guidance relates only to<br>rheumatoid arthritis and not to other forms of<br>arthritis, such as juvenile idiopathic arthritis and<br>psoriatic arthritis (see FAD sections 4.3.2 – 4.3.3).|



Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD Page 48 of 153

|---|---|---|
|Arthritis Care|The fact that the side effects of anti-TNF treatment can also be quite<br>significant is another reason why people with RA should be allowed to try<br>more than one – this is in fact the basis on which many PCTs operate.|Comment noted. Following the consultation on the<br>preliminary guidance the recommendations have<br>changed. For people who are contraindicated to<br>either rituximab or methotrexate or require rituximab<br>treatment be withdrawn because of an adverse<br>event, adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab and<br>abatacept are recommended as treatment options<br>(see FAD section 1).|
|Arthritis Care|The decision to allow rituximab but not anti-TNFs for sequential use is not<br>based on good evidence and appears to have been made without due<br>consideration of the context and effect on patient pathways. Following the consultation on the<br>preliminary guidance the recommendations have<br>changed. For people who are contraindicated to<br>either rituximab or methotrexate or require rituximab<br>treatment be withdrawn because of an adverse<br>event, adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab and<br>abatacept are recommended as treatment options<br>(see FAD section 1).|
|Arthritis Care|Under the preliminary findings, the only way patients would be able to try<br>more than one anti-TNF would be by entering into a clinical trial, which<br>clearly would be available only to a tiny fraction of people with RA. This<br>would also lead to people choosing to enter into clinical trials for the wrong<br>reasons.|Comment noted. Following the consultation on the<br>preliminary guidance the recommendations have<br>changed. For people who are contraindicated to<br>either rituximab or methotrexate or require rituximab<br>treatment be withdrawn because of an adverse<br>event, adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab and<br>abatacept are recommended as treatment options<br>(see FAD section 1).The guidance no longer<br>includes an “only in research” recommendation.|
|Arthritis Care|**Cost effectiveness**<br>The figures mentioned above demonstrate that any cost saving achieved by<br>a restrictive - and short-sighted - approach to the sequential use of anti-<br>TNFs will be very quickly and very clearly outweighed by the numerous<br>negative implications which this decision would have, not only for people<br>with RA but for the NHS and the UK economy.|Comment noted. The directions from the Secretary<br>of State for Health requests the Institute to make<br>recommendations to the NHS based on both clinical<br>and cost effectiveness. The appraisal has been<br>completed in accordance with the published guide<br>to the methods of technology appraisal including a<br>perspective of the NHS and PSS (see methods<br>guide sections 5.5, 6.1.3).|



Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 50 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|Arthritis Care|People denied clinically effective anti-TNF treatments will not cease<br>requiring treatment or accessing NHS services. On the contrary, if denied a<br>treatment which could slow the progress of the disease, many people will<br>inevitably rely much more heavily on NHS resources, including, for example,<br>cases where lack of appropriate treatment leads people with RA to require<br>expensive – and preventable – joint surgery, and greater use of palliative<br>care.|Comment noted. Models considered by the<br>Committee included costs associated with palliative<br>care and joint replacement surgery. The model by<br>the Assessment Group suggests that the estimates<br>of cost effectiveness were not very sensitive to<br>changing cost assumptions about hospitalisation<br>and joint replacement and palliative care (see FAD<br>section 4.3.15).|
|Arthritis Care|It is important to take a broad view of the costs involved, beyond the<br>financial costs to secondary care. The NAO has clearly highlighted that non-<br>biological treatment of RA carries significant costs to primary and secondary<br>care, in addition to the person with RA. On the other hand, recent evidence<br>compiled by the NAO shows that biological treatment of RA saves money,<br>e.g. in terms of reduced emergency admissions and less reliance on the<br>health sector generally.<br>The NAO has also developed an economic model in connection to its<br>aforementioned report on services for people with RA.  This model states<br>that the analyses conducted “have provided clear evidence that better value<br>for money could be achieved by providing more rapid treatment for people<br>with early onset rheumatoid arthritis,” improving patients’ quality of life and<br>delivering productivity gains for the economy.<br>The document goes on to say that “although it could increase the cost to the<br>NHS in the short-term, it would be cost effective, and could be cost saving in<br>the longer term”. Finally, it states that “the analyses also confirm the NICE<br>conclusion that intensive early treatment with step-down strategy is more<br>cost effective than current routine practice in terms of sequential DMARD<br>treatment (which is dominated by mono switch treatment strategy), and<br>suggest that potential cost savings to the NHS could be realised in the<br>medium to long-term.”|Comments noted. The National Audit Office (NAO)<br>analysis focused on the cost effectiveness of<br>providing earlier treatment and diagnosis of<br>rheumatoid arthritis, incorporating published NICE<br>guidance for TNF inhibitors to reflect the treatment<br>pathway for established disease. The current<br>appraisal starts at the point after the failure of a<br>TNF inhibitor. Therefore these two analyses<br>respond to different questions and their conclusions<br>may differ.|



Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 51 -->

Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|Arthritis Care|**Conclusion**<br>Arthritis Care feels strongly that the preliminary findings do not reflect the<br>existing medical evidence, expert clinical opinion and patient views, and are<br>not at all in the best interests of people with RA.<br>Where a clinically effective treatment is available, it is unacceptable – and<br>medically pointless - to deny people with RA this option, forcing them to<br>return to treatments which they and their health professionals know to be<br>ineffective.<br>Crucially, being able to access the best treatments – and find the anti-TNF<br>treatment which works for each individual person with RA – helps to keep<br>people independent, allows them to remain in or return to work, and<br>ultimately saves the NHS and the UK economy vast sums of money.<br>The outcome of this long and difficult appraisal process must not be another<br>“prescription for pain”. This would be perverse, counterproductive and self-<br>defeating. On the contrary, it must be an outcome which has the best<br>interests of people with RA at heart.<br>We therefore urge NICE to review its preliminary findings in light of the<br>information in this document, taking much greater account of not only the<br>existing clinical evidence for the need for a wide availability of anti-TNF<br>treatment, but also of patient experience and patient choice as a<br>fundamental and essential driver of decisions regarding people’s health.<br>Key to the above is to base any decision on anti-TNF treatment on a<br>genuine, open and honest discussion with a wide range of key stakeholders,<br>including clinicians, people with RA and user-led organisations.|Comment noted. The directions from the Secretary<br>of State for Health requests the Institute to make<br>recommendations to the NHS based on both clinical<br>and cost effectiveness. The Committee considered<br>all of the evidence submitted, which included<br>statements from clinical specialists and patient<br>experts about the impact of rheumatoid arthritis and<br>the importance of the availability of medications<br>(see FAD sections 4.3.2 – 4.3.4).<br>Following the consultation on the preliminary<br>guidance the recommendations have changed. For<br>people who are contraindicated to either rituximab<br>or methotrexate or require rituximab treatment be<br>withdrawn because of an adverse event,<br>adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab and abatacept<br>are recommended as treatment options (see FAD<br>section 1).|

The figures are very comparable, with 57% (nearly 30%<br>in the UK wide survey) of people who have lost their job due to their RA,<br>losing it within 1 year of diagnosis and 80% losing their job within 6 years of<br>diagnosis (59% in respect of the UK wide survey). 80% of people in the<br>recent Scotland survey said that fatigue was the biggest barrier to remain in<br>work and yet this is not adequately reflected in HAQ. >65% said that pain<br>was the biggest barrier to remaining in work and this, equally, is not<br>adequately reflected in HAQ. It is no longer a supportable position to take for<br>NICE to simply say that these costs are ‘not within their remit’. NICE should<br>be lobbying government to change their remit to reflect wider societal costs<br>in their economic modelling. The figures in the economic modelling<br>contained in the NAO report and the work of Dame Carol Black support this.|Comment noted. The framework for this appraisal is<br>the NICE 2008 methods guide. The appraisal has<br>been completed in accordance with this. As per the<br>NICE reference case (see sections 5.2.7 to 5.2.10<br>of the NICE methods guide), costs incurred outside<br>the NHS or PSS (such as those owing to time away<br>from work) were not incorporated. The Committee<br>recognised that factors such as pain may not be<br>adequately recognised in HAQ score (see FAD<br>section 4.3.17).<br>The model in the National Audit Office (NAO) report<br>focused on the treatment of early rheumatoid<br>arthritis, incorporating published NICE guidance for<br>TNF inhibitors to reflect treatment for established<br>disease. The Committee recognised the<br>impact of symptoms on the lives of people with<br>rheumatoid arthritis (see FAD sections 2.3 and<br>4.3.2). |Rheumatoid arthritis<br>Abbott<br>Laboratories|- Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee<br>**Comment continued from previous page**<br>Abbott argues that there are two ways of treating a patient with rituximab<br>and therefore two ways in which it can be modelled. Either patients are re-<br>treated when their disease flares and thus the modelling should take into<br>account likely higher HAQ progression as a result of losing efficacy; or<br>patients are re-treated to maintain tight disease control, which necessitates<br>using a 6 month re-treatment window. Abbott considers that if an 8.7 month<br>re-treatment window is assumed in the BRAM, the base case analysis for<br>the model should be re-run with a greater HAQ progression for rituximab<br>than for TNF inhibitors to incorporate the impact of loss of disease control<br>with re-treatment every 8.7 months. It does not seem clinically appropriate to<br>let patients’ disease flare, therefore, Abbott suggests that the BRAM base<br>case analysis should apply a 6 month re-treatment window and use the<br>QALY gain derived from the 24 week HAQ improvements from REFLEX.<br>When this scenario is assumed, the ICERs for adalimumab and rituximab vs.<br>conventional DMARDs in the BRAM model are similar (£34,300 and<br>£32,600/QALY gained respectively; Table 19 of the Addendum report).<br>Furthermore, had the BRAM included a stopping rule for the TNF inhibitors,<br>as it should have done, then one-way sensitivity analysis using the BRAM<br>model shows that the ICER for adalimumab vs. conventional DMARDs<br>would<br>be<br>£22,200/QALY<br>gained<br>(Addendum<br>report).<br>Both<br>these<br>assumptions, when taken together, indicate that TNF inhibitors are likely to<br>be cost effective versus DMARDs and versus rituximab, and demonstrate<br>that to conclude only in favour of rituximab is unsound.<br>In the same vein, Abbott contends that its original base case assumption of<br>9 monthly re-treatment with rituximab is no longer appropriate in light of<br>recent trial evidence showing 6-monthly re-treatment is necessary to<br>maintain disease control. Results of a revised base case analysis using the<br>Abbott model assuming a 6 month re-treatment with rituximab demonstrates<br>comparable and stable cost effectiveness ratios (around £16,000/ QALY) for<br>TNF inhibitors and rituximab vs. DMARDs with a probability for the TNF<br>inhibitors to be cost effective over 50% of the time. In addition, TNF<br>inhibitors are also cost effective vs. rituximab (around £17,000/ QALY) and<br>estimates are fairly stable under various scenarios tested in the sensitivity<br>analyses.|, commentator and public comments on the ACD<br> <br>The Committee considered the length of time<br>between rituximab treatments in its deliberations. It<br>heard from clinical specialists that they would aim to<br>treat patients before disease flared, and disease<br>control was lost. However, there was wide variation<br>in time required between infusions. The Committee<br>concluded that while 8.7 months between<br>treatments may be an over estimate of the average<br>re-treatment interval, it would not be the case that<br>every person required rituximab re-treatment every<br>6 months. Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|<br>Abbott<br>Laboratories|<br>In a recent talk given by Professor Sir Michael Rawlins to the Royal College<br>of Physicians  Professor Rawlins argued that a new approach was needed<br>to analyse clinical evidence: “Randomised controlled trials (RCTs), long<br>regarded at the ‘gold standard' of evidence, have been put on an<br>undeserved pedestal.  Their appearance at the top of "hierarchies" of<br>evidence is inappropriate; and hierarchies, themselves, are illusory tools for<br>assessing evidence.  They should be replaced by a diversity of approaches<br>that involve analysing the totality of the evidence-base.” As outlined by<br>Professor Rawlins, there are several limitations with RCTs, and<br>observational studies are a useful source of information that with care in the<br>interpretation of the results, can provide an important source of evidence<br>about both the benefits and harms of therapeutic interventions not captured<br>by RCTs. Professor Rawlins comments that, “RCTs are often carried out on<br>specific types of patients for a relatively short period of time, whereas in<br>clinical practice the treatment will be used on a much greater variety of<br>patients - often suffering from other medical conditions - and for much<br>longer.” Therefore, it follows that registry data and observational studies<br>evaluating the effectiveness and safety of interventions in routine clinical<br>practice also have important information value in capturing the effectiveness<br>of an intervention in the patient population in which its use is intended.  As<br>such, data from the ReAct study evaluating the effectiveness of adalimumab<br>for rheumatoid arthritis in patients with a history of TNF-antagonist therapy in<br>clinical practice, and data from country specific registries like the British<br>Society for Rheumatology biologics Register (BSRBR), should be given due<br>weight in the Committee’s consideration of the evidence.<br>Furthermore, in section 3.2.8 of the NICE guide to the methods of<br>technology appraisal  it states that non-RCT data is required, “Non-RCT,<br>both experimental and observational, evidence will be required, not just for<br>those situations in which RCTs are unavailable, but also to supplement<br>information from RCTs when they are available.” The methods guide notes<br>that there is a greater problem of confounding and bias in non-RCT data and<br>section 3.2.9 of the guide therefore states: “When possible, the use of more<br>than one independent source of such evidence needs to be examined to<br>gain some assurance of the validity of any conclusions drawn.”<br>**Comment continued on next page**|<br>As indicated in the comment, the NICE guide to the<br>methods of technology appraisal recognises that<br>evidence from RCTs is not always available and<br>acknowledges that non-randomised data may be<br>required to supplement RCT data. However, the<br>methods guide also states that RCTs are<br>considered to provide the most valid evidence of<br>relative effectiveness and that inference drawn from<br>non-RCT evidence will necessarily be more<br>circumspect than those from RCTs with properly<br>controlled evidence (sections 3.2.5, 3.2.9).<br>The Committee considered the non-randomised<br>evidence identified by the Assessment Group and<br>submitted by consultees. The Committee accepted<br>that the data available suggested that a second<br>TNF inhibitor was effective. However, it considered<br>that it could not quantify with certainty the relative<br>effect of adalimumab, etanercept and infliximab in<br>comparison with conventional or biologic DMARDs<br>(see FAD sections 4.3.7, 4.3.8).|

However, the Committee<br>considered that it could not quantify with certainty<br>the relative effect of adalimumab, etanercept and<br>infliximab in comparison with conventional or<br>biologic DMARDs (see FAD sections 4.3.7, 4.3.8).<br>Following the consultation on the preliminary<br>guidance the recommendations have changed.<br>Rituximab is recommended as a treatment option<br>for people who do not respond adequately to a TNF<br>inhibitor. For people who have contraindications to<br>rituximab or methotrexate or who require that<br>rituximab treatment be withdrawn because of an<br>adverse event, adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab<br>and abatacept are recommended as treatment<br>options (see FAD section 1).|

Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|

|Abbott<br>Laboratories|While heterogeneity is clearly of concern, it is not a concern unique to meta-<br>analysis.  Indeed, meta-analysis is essentially observational in nature – the<br>context in which each datum is generated and the process by which that<br>datum is observed and reported is inherently complex and heterogeneous.<br>When estimating treatment effects in epidemiology or the social sciences,<br>one would rarely have the luxury of unconditional exchangeability between<br>individuals in treatment and control groups.  Hence the wide use of<br>regression analysis.  By casting a relatively wide evidentiary net, it is<br>possible to include observations from a variety of contexts and then use that<br>variability together with a statistical model to identify and at least partially<br>control for heterogeneity.  To do otherwise would be to throw away data<br>relevant to the decision-maker.  Such is the published view of academic<br>experts on evidence synthesis for cost-effectiveness modelling, including<br>individuals who have played important roles in developing  NICE<br>methodology for appraisal , :<br>_“A second issue about the evidence base for CE analysis is that there are_<br>_always likely to be multiple sources of evidence on particular parameters,_<br>_particularly on relative effectiveness. It is very rarely the case, for example,_<br>_that a single RCT represents the entirety of information about effectiveness._<br>_In reality, there are likely to be several trials and probably some_<br>_observational evidence. However these different sources are not likely to_<br>_relate to identical patient groups or clinical practice – in other words, they_<br>_exhibit heterogeneity. Such evidence may be indirect in various ways, but it_<br>_is clearly relevant and therefore cannot be excluded. To assess CE, all_<br>_available data should be incorporated into an analysis with explicit methods_<br>_used to reflect the heterogeneity and uncertainty in the evidence.”2_<br>**Comment continued on next page**|<br>Comments noted. The Committee considered the<br>use of relative and absolute treatment effects in the<br>economic models. It agreed that alternative<br>approaches to that of the Assessment Group<br>should not necessarily be discounted. The<br>Committee considered the methods through which<br>the clinical effectiveness data had been calculated<br>and incorporated into the submitted economic<br>analyses. It considered the range of estimates of<br>cost effectiveness and identified where the models<br>could lead to different conclusions about the cost<br>effectiveness of the treatments under appraisal.<br>The Committee did not consider that the different<br>methods of analysis of the clinical effectiveness<br>data altered its conclusions about the use of the<br>technologies in the NHS (See FAD sections 4.3.13,<br>4.3.18, 4.3.25).|
