|Abbott<br>Laboratories|This has been the approach taken in the Abbott MTC.  By adopting a broad<br>set of inclusion criteria, it is possible to borrow strength from a larger body of<br>evidence when RCT data strictly on the comparative efficacy of 2nd line<br>biologics in the treatment of RA are extremely limited.   Variation in study<br>settings and design allows for the exploration of several specific potential<br>sources of heterogeneity through the use of “mixed effects” meta-regression<br>modelling in an approach similar to an MTC meta-regression on RA<br>treatment published by Nixon and colleagues .  Furthermore, this approach<br>uses a single complete evidentiary network for estimation of all treatment<br>effects relevant to the appraisal.  Contrary to approaches where treatment<br>effects are estimated in separate analyses, this approach also obtains<br>meaningful ‘cross-parameter’ correlation of treatment effects, which can be<br>of critical importance to the inference obtained from probabilistic sensitivity<br>analysis:<br>_“Firstly, cost-effectiveness analyses need to be based on all the available_<br>_evidence, not a selected subset, and the uncertainties in the data need to be_<br>_propagated through the model in order to provide a correct analysis of the_<br>_uncertainties in the decision. In many--perhaps most--cases the evidence_<br>_structure requires a statistical analysis that inevitably induces correlations_<br>_between parameters.”3 _<br>**Comment continued on next page**|Comments noted. |**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|

|Abbott<br>Laboratories|**2.1**<br>**Cost-effectiveness of anti-TNFs vs. conventional DMARDs**<br>**2.1.1 Implication of the presumed effectiveness of conventional**<br>**DMARDs on the cost-effectiveness estimates**<br>In section 4.3.6 and 4.3.21 of the ACD, the Committee noted that the BRAM<br>assumed that conventional DMARDs used after the failure of a TNF inhibitor<br>were 50% as effective as when used in early rheumatoid arthritis. The<br>Committee considered that in light of the clinical experts’ testimony<br>regarding the poor efficacy of conventional DMARDs at this point in the<br>treatment pathway, the Assessment Group may have overestimated the<br>efficacy of conventional DMARDs and as a result overestimated the ICERs<br>in the base case analysis.<br>In the Assessment Group’s addendum, scenario analysis using efficacy<br>estimates for DMARDs comparable to placebo shows that the ICER for<br>adalimumab vs. conventional DMARDs would be about £28,100/QALY<br>gained. Whilst the Committee concluded that an analysis that assumed the<br>effect of conventional DMARDs to be no more than that of placebo was not<br>plausible, it should be noted that the placebo analysis is derived from RA<br>patients from the REFLEX or ATTAIN randomised controlled trials who have<br>failed a TNF inhibitor and who were receiving a DMARD - methotrexate. It is<br>therefore not unreasonable to assume that conventional DMARDs would be<br>about as effective as ‘placebo’ at this stage in the treatment pathway, in line<br>with data from the BSRBR for patients stopping TNF inhibitor therapy (0<br>HAQ improvement for patients stopping a TNF inhibitor and going back onto<br>conventional DMARDs). In the ‘poor DMARD response’ scenario, the<br>probability that adalimumab would be a cost-effective treatment option at a<br>willingness to pay threshold of £30,000/QALY would increase from 30% in<br>the Assessment Group’s base case to 57%.<br>In section 4.3.10 of the ACD, the Committee concluded that,_“Overall, on the_<br>_basis of clinical opinion, the effect of conventional DMARDs in people for_<br>_whom a TNF inhibitor had failed was likely to be small, but the relative effect_<br>_in comparison with biological treatments was not currently quantifiable.”_<br>**Comment continued on next page**|The Committee considered that the assumed 50%<br>reduction in efficacy of conventional DMARDs to be<br>an underestimate of the reduction in efficacy, when<br>conventional DMARDs are used in established as<br>opposed to early disease. However, it did not<br>accept that there would be no effect at all<br>associated with therapy (see FAD sections 4.3.12<br>and 4.3.23). The Committee considered the ICERs<br>reflecting a greater reduction in conventional<br>DMARD efficacy in their deliberations.<br>Both the REFLEX and ATTAIN trials are placebo<br>controlled trials in which rituximab or abatacept are<br>added to background methotrexate or conventional<br>DMARDs. Conventional DMARDs had to be given<br>at a stable dose prior to randomisation. These<br>studies demonstrate the effect of rituximab and<br>abatacept in comparison to placebo rather than the<br>effect of treatment with a conventional DMARD.|



Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 82 -->

## Confidential until publication 

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|Abbott<br>Laboratories|As has been extensively discussed in previous correspondence on this<br>issue, there is a paucity of evidence available for the effectiveness of<br>conventional DMARDs in a TNF inhibitor failure population. This data gap is<br>not only wide for patients failing a TNF inhibitor, it also exists for patients<br>failing two prior DMARDs as no randomised controlled trials have<br>considered the effectiveness of conventional DMARDs after failure of two<br>DMARDs in patients with established/ late RA with many years of disease<br>duration. One of the consequences of the lack of data on the effectiveness<br>of conventional DMARDs in later lines of therapy is that it is difficult to<br>precisely quantify the cost effectiveness of all biologic therapies versus<br>conventional DMARDs. The outcome of this uncertainty could be the<br>restriction of biologic therapies leading to use of conventional DMARDs in<br>anti-TNF failure populations with minimal effect. As one option, given the<br>absence of appropriate clinical trial data for conventional DMARDs, it may<br>be instructive to assess their effectiveness using observational data. The<br>limited observational data from the BROSG and BSRBR studies indicate that<br>sequential use of conventional DMARDs after methotrexate failure in late RA<br>does not significantly improve HAQ scores in either the short term or long<br>term.<br>Although the populations in the above studies do not adequately reflect the<br>anti-TNF failure population, given that sequences of conventional DMARDs<br>have not been able to reduce HAQ scores in studies of late RA it is highly<br>unlikely that this would be possible in the more severe anti-TNF failure<br>population (who have failed two or more DMARDs prior to failing their first<br>TNF inhibitor). Therefore, cost-effectiveness estimates used in the<br>Committee’s decision making should be based on the limited clinical effect of<br>DMARDs in this patient group as the most plausible estimates, and not on<br>the Assessment Group’s arbitrary 50% reduction in effectiveness from an<br>early RA study.|The Committee considered that the assumed 50%<br>reduction in efficacy of conventional DMARDs to be<br>an underestimate of the reduction in efficacy, when<br>conventional DMARDs are used in established as<br>opposed to early disease. However, it did not<br>accept that there would be no effect at all<br>associated with therapy (see FAD sections 4.3.12<br>and 4.3.23). The Committee considered the ICERs<br>reflecting a greater reduction in conventional<br>DMARD efficacy in their deliberations.|

However, in the Addendum<br>report, the Assessment Group did conduct a scenario analysis in which a<br>proportion of patients stopped treatment due to non-response after 6 months<br>of therapy based on the Abbott model stopping rule of an ACR50 response.<br>This analysis reduced the ICER for adalimumab vs. conventional DMARDs<br>to £22,200/QALY gained.  Unfortunately the Assessment Group did not<br>present the probability that each drug would be cost-effective at various<br>thresholds for this scenario analysis.<br>The Assessment Group’s reason for not including a stopping rule based on<br>response criteria stemmed from BSRBR data indicating that a number of<br>people continue treatment with a TNF inhibitor even in the absence of such<br>a response. The Committee<br>concluded that continuation rules should be<br>considered in the estimation of cost effectiveness<br>(see FAD section 4.3.22).|

In the ACD, the Committee accepts the fact that the effect of<br>conventional DMARDs in people for whom a TNF inhibitor had failed is likely<br>to be small, and that a stopping rule based on response criteria should be<br>used to determine whether patients should continue treatment. |Abbott<br>Laboratories|**2.2**<br>**Interpretation of the evidence for the cost-effectiveness of**<br>**rituximab**<br>In section 4.3.19 of the ACD, the Committee noted that, “The BRAM<br>modelled time to repeat treatment as 8.7 months in the base case, basing<br>this estimate on Roche’s submission. It noted that similar time to re-<br>treatment had been assumed in a number of the other manufacturers’<br>submissions. On the basis of the clinical specialists’ advice, the Committee<br>assumed that treatment with rituximab would occur, on average, less<br>frequently than every 6 months.” It states elsewhere in the ACD that the<br>cost-effectiveness estimates are very sensitive to the re-treatment window<br>applied to rituximab; and the conclusions from this statement imply that as<br>re-treatment occurs less frequently than every 6 months, rituximab is a cost-<br>effective use of NHS resources. However, the BRAM used a re-treatment<br>window of 8.7 months but applied 6 month HAQ changes from REFLEX. The<br>only other model to use an 8.7 month re-treatment window was the Abbott<br>model but this included the following caveat: “The results represent an<br>optimistic estimate of the cost-effectiveness of rituximab with regards to<br>assumptions around the re-dosing interval”. Since the evidence submission<br>in August 2009, data from the SUNRISE trial  and the change to the FDA<br>labelling for rituximab indicate that a re-treatment window of 6 months would<br>have been more appropriate in the Abbott base case analysis.<br>**Comment continued on next page**|The Committee considered the length of time<br>between rituximab treatments in its deliberations. However, there was wide variation<br>in time required between infusions. The Committee<br>concluded that while 8.7 months between<br>treatments may be an over estimate of the average<br>re-treatment interval, it would not be the case that<br>every person required rituximab re-treatment every<br>6 months. The guidance for rituximab includes a rule<br>that rituximab is only continued if an adequate<br>response can be maintained following retreatment<br>with a dosing interval of at least 6 months (see FAD<br>section 4.3.21).|

|**Consultee**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|Rheumatoid arthritis<br>Abbott<br>Laboratories|- Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee<br>**2.2.1 Evidence supporting loss of efficacy for rituximab when > 6**<br>**month re-treatment interval is used**<br>The current EMA marketing authorisation for rituximab does not give any<br>guidance as to the time period between treatments for rheumatoid arthritis,<br>simply the minimum time between re-treatment (16 weeks). However, in<br>June 2009 the manufacturer of rituximab filed a variation to the EMA seeking<br>approval for first line biologic use of rituximab in RA patients who have failed<br>conventional DMARD therapy. The data supporting this variation are based<br>on the MIRROR and SERENE trials which all specified re-treatment with<br>rituximab starting at 24 weeks for patients with a DAS28 score ≥ 2.6. Given<br>that the patients in these trials had not failed a prior TNF inhibitor, then this<br>suggests that re-treatment with rituximab in a more refractory patient<br>population who have failed a TNF inhibitor is likely to be at least every 24<br>weeks to ensure maintenance of response. Furthermore, in February 2010<br>the US FDA label for the use of rituximab in RA patients who have failed a<br>TNF inhibitor was amended to the following based on newly available clinical<br>evidence: “Subsequent courses of rituximab should be administered every<br>24 weeks or based on clinical evaluation, but not sooner than every 16<br>weeks.”<br>Keystone et al assessed the DAS28 score of patients prior to re-treatment<br>with rituximab . In this open-label extension study, patients were enrolled<br>from three rituximab phase II and III trials in patients previously treated with<br>TNF inhibitors. They were eligible for repeated courses of rituximab based<br>on certain criteria: a <20% reduction in tender and swollen joint count from<br>baseline, with associated active disease defined as >8 tender and swollen<br>joints present. Clinical efficacy, as measured by DAS28, was analysed at 24<br>weeks (see Figure 2.2.1.1) but the median time between courses of re-<br>treatment was 38 weeks (course 1 to 2) and 42 weeks (course 2 to 3). In the<br>period between 24 weeks and re-treatment with the next course, the DAS28<br>demonstrates a poor clinical response with return to near baseline values.<br>The mean DAS28 just prior to course 1 was 7.01 and just prior to course 2<br>re-treatment was 6.17, or a reduction of 0.84, showing that patients are not<br>maintaining clinical response.<br>**Figure included, but not reproduced here**<br>**Comment continued on next page**|, commentator and public comments on the ACD<br> <br>The current marketing authorisation for rituximab<br>does not specify a re-treatment interval. However,<br>labels from other regulatory agencies do not inform<br>the appraisal, although the evidence leading to the<br>amendment may do so if submitted.<br>The Committee considered the length of time<br>between rituximab treatments in its deliberations. It<br>concluded that while 8.7 months between<br>treatments may be an over estimate of the re-<br>treatment interval, it would not be the case that<br>every person required rituximab re-treatment every<br>6 months (see FAD section 4.3.21).|

In this 559 patient trial, all patients were given rituximab at<br>week 0; at week 24 those patients not in remission (DAS28 < 2.6) were then<br>randomised in a 2:1 ratio to receive a second course of rituximab or placebo.<br>Approximately 85% of patients were not in DAS28 remission at week 24 and<br>were randomised; although it is not clear whether the 15% of patients not<br>randomised were actually in remission or whether they were lost to follow-up<br>as the paper does not report how missing data were handled. This suggests that a large proportion of patients<br>are losing response between weeks 24 and 28, and are not regaining it i.e.<br>there does not seem to be as much benefit from a 2nd course of rituximab<br>for the group who lose response between weeks 24 and 28.  The authors of<br>this study concluded that because the goals of re-treatment include<br>maintenance of efficacy and prevention of flare, re-treatment should occur<br>prior to worsening, and therefore Week 24 appeared to be an appropriate<br>time to re-treat in most patients.|The Committee recommended a stopping rule<br>whereby treatment with rituximab is stopped if<br>treatment is required more frequently than every 6<br>months. |Abbott<br>Laboratories|**2.3.2 Safety of treatment with rituximab in RA patients**<br>The safety of rituximab needs to be given due consideration in this appraisal<br>considering<br>the<br>increased<br>risk<br>of<br>Progressive<br>Multifocal<br>Leukoencephaolopathy (PML) in RA patients receiving rituximab detailed in<br>the SmPC . In September 2009, Genentech and the FDA notified healthcare<br>professionals about a case of PML in a patient receiving treatment with<br>rituximab for rheumatoid arthritis (the patient had not previously been treated<br>with methotrexate or a TNF inhibitor)  . This represents the third fatal case of<br>PML in an RA patient receiving rituximab which now has a black box safety<br>warning regarding the infectious demyelinating condition , . Interestingly,<br>rituximab treatment has also been associated with the development of PML<br>in a number of other conditions: in a recent publication 52 patients with<br>lymphoid malignancies, 2 patients with SLE, 1 patient with rheumatoid<br>arthritis, 1 patient with idiopathic autoimmune pancytopenia, and 1 patient<br>with immune thrombocytopenia purpura all developed PML after rituximab<br>treatment . The case fatality rate was 90% for these patients. As of July 29,<br>2008, there were 76 reports in the manufacturer’s global safety database of<br>confirmed or suspected PML in patients receiving rituximab in any indication<br>. This further highlights the need for increased awareness and reporting of<br>rituximab-associated PML cases in order to improve our understanding of<br>the risk factors, natural course, and alternative therapeutic approaches.<br>Overall, the reported incidence of PML in patients with RA receiving<br>rituximab is rare (3 reports in approximately 100,000 RA patients on<br>rituximab). Table 9.1 and Table 9.2 of the Addendum states that 6<br>doses of infliximab are given per year, with one additional dose in the first<br>year. The licence for infliximab states that treatment should be administered<br>at week 0, 2 and 6 and then every 8 weeks thereafter. It is clear from the<br>dosing assumptions for adalimumab and etanercept that the Assessment<br>Group assumes a 52 week year. As stated in NICE’s costing template for<br>TA130, this corresponds to 8 doses in the first year, and either 6 or 7 doses<br>per year thereafter (i.e. 6.5 doses on average). |Abbott<br>Laboratories|**Do you consider that the provisional recommendations of the**<br>**Appraisal Committee are sound and constitute a suitable basis for the**<br>**preparation of guidance to the NHS?**<br>Abbott considers that the provisional recommendations do not constitute a<br>suitable basis for the preparation of guidance to the NHS because the<br>recommendations do not take into account the need for a sequence of<br>biologic therapy options for patients with severe RA with very low quality of<br>life. A significant proportion of TNF failure patients have pain, fatigue and<br>functional impairment which the general population views as so severe that<br>they consider these states as worse than death, highlighting the severity of<br>this patient population.<br>At present it is unknown which patients will respond to a particular biologic<br>therapy and, at the individual level, patients show a significant heterogeneity<br>of response, such that a patient responding poorly to a first TNF inhibitor<br>could have a markedly greater response to a 2nd TNF inhibitor. If the<br>provisional recommendations were to become guidance to the NHS, UK<br>patients would not get the opportunity to receive a 3rd or 4th biologic<br>treatment option, which at the individual patient level could deny the patient<br>the chance of an improved quality of life. Given uncertainties regarding the effectiveness of rituximab in<br>rheumatoid factor negative patients, the safety of biologic treatment after<br>rituximab and the similar cost of TNF inhibitors and rituximab when rituximab<br>re-treatment is given every 6 months, as necessary to maintain disease<br>control, Abbott considers it is inappropriate to recommend rituximab as the<br>only biologic option for patients failing a TNF inhibitor.|The Committee specifically considered the<br>subgroup of people who test seronegative. A sensitivity analysis conducted by the<br>Assessment Group found that partial incorporation of a stopping rule based<br>on response criterion within the BRAM lowered the ICERs by approximately<br>£10,000 / QALY gained. This coupled with a belief that the<br>use of stopping rules does not reflect current clinical practice led to the<br>Committee’s conclusion that this component could not be the basis for<br>decision making.<br>Schering-Plough believes that the Committee’s view set out in section 4.3.20<br>of the ACD is perverse for the following reasons:<br>•<br>Firstly, it is our understanding that the majority of Primary Care<br>Trusts (“PCTs”) will audit the use of TNF inhibitors and will require data on<br>response.  No evidence that we are aware of has been presented to<br>substantiate the view that there is widespread refusal to stop TNF inhibitor<br>therapy where patients have not responded.<br>•<br>Secondly, the Institute’s own guidance as set out in TA130 requires<br>that response to treatment is determined and non-responders are withdrawn<br>from therapy. Modelling cost-effectiveness must therefore take this into<br>account.<br>•<br>Thirdly, it is unacceptable for the Committee to dismiss the relevant<br>cost-effectiveness estimates on the basis that response criteria are only<br>partially incorporated – clearly the appropriate course of action is to demand<br>that the BRAM is amended to allow for this clinically important element of<br>therapy to be fully incorporated, particularly since it appears that cost-<br>effectiveness estimates are likely to be sensitive to this assumption.<br>**Comment continued on next page**|The Committee heard from clinical specialists that<br>although implementing stopping rules could be<br>difficult, clinicians were increasingly following<br>guidance on stopping rules. The Committee<br>concluded that continuation rules should be<br>considered in the estimation of cost effectiveness<br>(see FAD section 4.3.22).|

Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD 




<!-- page 119 -->

Confidential until publication 

**Commentator Comment** UCB Pharma **Second line use of anti-TNFs vs. rituximab – table 2** Similarly, the results indicate that regardless of the first-line therapy used, in second line use the ICER for CZP vs rituximab (range: £31,000 – 35,000) is lower than the ICERs for all the other three anti-TNFs vs rituximab (range: £400,000 to dominated). 

It should be noted that the results presented above only consider a 6-month stopping rule. If a 3-month stopping rule is employed with certolizumab rather than a 6-month stopping rule, non-responders to certolizumab would come off treatment earlier, making the results more favourable towards certolizumab than those presented above. As has been outlined, certolizumab efficacy can be assessed at 3 months (12 weeks) and so no patients would progress and then fail at 6 months. All the other TNFs have a 6-month initial review period.1, 2 

These results have not been presented here as we have only modelled the PAS as a cost saving option over the first three months. This has been done to allow proper comparison between each TNF inhibitor option. If we had applied a 3-month stopping rule to certolizumab and a different 6-months stopping rule to the other TNF inhibitors, the QALY for certolizumab would improve, the cost base would reduce and the ICER for certolizumab against the other TNF inhibitors would be further improved. 

## **Response** 

Comments noted. The Committee discussed the inclusion of certolizumab pegol as a comparator in this appraisal (see FAD section 4.3.5). Certolizumab pegol was subject to its own single technology appraisal (see NICE technology appraisal guidance 186). It was therefore not subject to appraisal by the Committee in this instance (see FAD section 4.3.5). It was included as a comparator in the scope of this appraisal, but the Committee is unable to make recommendations about comparators. The guidance in this appraisal relates only to the use of adalimumab, etanercept and infliximab and not to the TNF inhibitors more generally. TA186 for certolizumab pegol does not make recommendations about sequential use. In the absence of recommendations the use of certolizumab pegol after the failure of a first TNF inhibitor is subject to local decision making.

It concluded that while 8.7 months between<br>treatments may be an over estimate of the re-<br>treatment interval, it would not be the case that<br>every person required rituximab re-treatment every<br>6 months (see FAD section 4.3.21).<br>The Committee is able to make recommendations<br>within the marketing authorisation that enable a<br>cost effective use of the technology. It concluded<br>that rituximab could be considered cost effective as<br>long as infusions were not required more frequently<br>than every six months. (see FAD section 4.3.21)|
|NHS Quality<br>Improvement<br>Scotland –<br>Expert 1|_Are the provisional recommendations of the Appraisal Committee sound and_<br>_do they constitute a suitable basis for the preparation of guidance to the_<br>_NHS? If not, why do you consider that the recommendations are not sound?_<br>I consider these recommendations to be unsound because they do not take<br>into sufficient consideration the opinions of clinical specialists who are highly<br>experienced in the management of patients with RA. It is accepted that there<br>is an inadequate research basis on which to make this recommendation and<br>because of this, greater weight should have been placed on best practice. In<br>addition the recommendation is already out of date because it does not take<br>into consideration Tocilizumab (TOC). If this recommendation is to go<br>forward it should be with a predetermined short review date so as to be able<br>to incorporate emerging data on the use of Rituximab in seropositive vs<br>seronegative patients aswellas the placing of TOCinthe pathway.|The Committee considered all of the evidence<br>submitted, which included statements from clinical<br>specialists and patient experts. In addition patient<br>exerts and clinical specialists attended the<br>Committee meeting to provide expert advice (see<br>FAD sections 4.3.2 – 4.3.4).<br>Tocilizumab is currently subject to its own single<br>technology appraisal. It was therefore not subject to<br>appraisal by the Committee in this instance (see<br>FAD section 4.3.5)..|

|NHS Quality<br>Improvement<br>Scotland –<br>Expert 2|_Please add any other information which you think would be useful to NICE or_<br>_helpful in guiding the Scottish response to this assessment_<br>It might be worth commenting on “stopping rules” which are discussed in the<br>ACD. In general, stopping rules are adhered to in Scottish practice, though<br>with varying degrees of rigour depending_inter alia_on whether viable options<br>are available. Expert comment has indicated that there is little confidence in<br>the use of “untried conventional DMARD” in this context. |NHS Quality<br>Improvement<br>Scotland –<br>Expert 3|_Do you consider that all the relevant evidence has been taken into account?_<br>Yes<br>_Do you consider that the summaries of clinical and cost effectiveness are_<br>_reasonable interpretations of the evidence?_I continue to have reservations<br>about the BRAM model, particularly with respect to:<br>•<br>Its’ failure to incorporate stopping rules into the model. Whilst there is<br>some evidence from the BSRBR  that some patients continue on treatment<br>despite a failure to respond, these data are 1) limited by the nature of the<br>BSRBR, which was not designed to collect disease activity or drug response<br>data 2) of questionable relevance – the Committee questions whether the<br>application of response criteria would be ‘reflective of clinical practice’ (p44).<br>I would submit that the Committee should assess the cost effectiveness of<br>therapy according to best practice. Experience of clinicians around the<br>country shows that PCTs and HBs are increasingly auditing the use of anti-<br>TNF carefully, and that drug continuation in the absence of response will be<br>increasingly rare.<br>•<br>Its over-optimistic assessment of the value of DMARD therapy in<br>patients who have failed biologic therapy. The Committee recognise that the<br>BRAM model probably over-estimates the magnitude of response to<br>conventional DMARDs but it has not explored the issue of treatment<br>longevity with conventional DMARDs. The assumptions about the duration of<br>benefit from conventional DMARDs  used by the BRAM model are not<br>credible.|Comments noted, no actions required.<br>The Committee heard from clinical specialists that<br>although implementing stopping rules could be<br>difficult, clinicians were increasingly following<br>guidance on stopping rules. The Committee<br>concluded that continuation rules should be<br>considered in the estimation of cost effectiveness<br>(see FAD section 4.3.22).<br>The Committee considered that the assumed 50%<br>reduction in efficacy of conventional DMARDs to be<br>an underestimate of the reduction in efficacy, when<br>conventional DMARDs are used in established as<br>opposed to early disease. However, it did not<br>accept that there would be no effect at all<br>associated with therapy (see FAD sections 4.3.12<br>and 4.3.23). The Committee considered the ICERs<br>reflecting a greater reduction in conventional<br>DMARD efficacy in their deliberations.|



Rheumatoid arthritis - Adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, rituximab and abatacept - Response to consultee, commentator and public comments on the ACD

|**Commentator**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|NHS Quality<br>Improvement<br>Scotland –<br>Expert 3|_Are the provisional recommendations of the Appraisal Committee sound and_<br>_do they constitute a suitable basis for the preparation of guidance to the_<br>_NHS?_<br>Not, in my opinion, for the following reasons:<br>•<br>The Committee only considered anti-TNF therapy or abatacept as<br>alternatives to rituximab. The conclusion that rituximab is the most cost-<br>effective option for patients who fail anti-TNF therapy is probably correct, but<br>a significant proportion of patients will fail to respond to rituximab. Leaving<br>such patients without an option for further biologic therapy will generate<br>significant unmet need and this will be associated with considerable<br>personal hardship and suffering for the patients involved.<br>•<br>The Committee has given insufficient attention to that sub-group of<br>patients which responds very well to therapy. The ICER for each of the<br>drugs changes dramatically if the stopping rules change – so for instance, if<br>patients were required to have a larger response in order to stay on<br>treatment (for example by achieving a DAS28<3.2) it is probable that this<br>would represent cost effective treatment. The evidence suggests that some<br>patients do respond very well, for example, to abatacept following failure of<br>an anti-TNF drug and the Committee should explore a risk-sharing scheme<br>with the companies involved such that patients would be granted a trial of<br>therapy free of charge, with the NHS only paying for subsequent therapy in<br>patients with a good response.|Following the consultation on the preliminary<br>guidance the recommendations have changed. For<br>those who are contraindicated to either rituximab or<br>methotrexate or require rituximab treatment be<br>withdrawn because of an adverse event,<br>adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab and abatacept<br>are recommended as treatment options (see FAD<br>section 1). The Committee was not presented with<br>any evidence that enabled to it make<br>recommendations about the use of adalimumab,<br>etanercept, infliximab and abatacept after the failure<br>of rituximab (see FAD section 4.3.28).<br>The Committee heard from clinical specialists that<br>although implementing stopping rules could be<br>difficult, clinicians were increasingly following<br>guidance on stopping rules. The Committee<br>concluded that continuation rules should be<br>considered in the estimation of cost effectiveness<br>(see FAD section 4.3.22).<br>It is not within the Committee’s remit to engage in<br>price negotiation (including the initiation of a patient<br>access scheme). The manufacturer may submit a<br>patient access scheme to the Department of Health.|
|NHS Quality<br>Improvement<br>Scotland –<br>Expert 3|_Are the patient pathways and treatment options described in the assessment_<br>_applicable to NHSScotland?_Yes, although tocilizumab is also approved for<br>use in NHS Scotland in patients who fail anti-TNF therapy.|Comments noted. Tocilizumab is currently subject<br>to its own single technology appraisal. It was<br>therefore not subject to appraisal by the Committee<br>in this instance (see FAD section 4.3.5).|

|**Commentator**|**Comment**|**Response**|
|---|---|---|
|Arthritis<br>Research UK|Having read this I find the health economic arguments difficult to follow.<br>However, from the academic perspective the main comment I have is that<br>they have not referred to the 'GO-AFTER' study or ATTEST study (both<br>attached).<br>GO-AFTER was an RCT in which patients who had 'failed' MTX were<br>randomised to either placebo or one of two doses of golimumab.  Although<br>golimumab is not NICE approved, I think the results of this study could be<br>extrapolated to other mAb TNF inhibitors.<br>39,19 and 11% of patients achieved ACR 20, 50 and 70 at 24 weeks<br>compared to 17, 5 and 3% of PBO patients.  Although these numbers look<br>quite low, patients did not need to be taking MTX and acute phase response<br>could be normal at baseline.  I guess this makes the study difficult to<br>compare to the RTX and ABA studies after anti-TNF. (Incidentally, I cannot<br>understand how this paper made it into the Lancet).<br>The other arguably relevant paper is the ATTEST study (also attached).<br>This was a study of abatacept or infliximab vs PBO.  It was not a head-to-<br>head of abatacept vs infliximab but patients were randomised to either drug<br>or PBO (3:3:2).  At one year both drugs were superior to PBO.  Abatacept<br>patients fared numerically better than INF patients (across all domains,<br>including HAQ-DI) and had fewer AEs and SAEs.  Although these were MTX<br>IRs, one could argue that the INF arm would have performed relatively<br>worse if this had been a TNF-IR study.  Thus, whilst not a head-to-head, the<br>therapeutic ratio looked somewhat better for abatacept.<br>Whilst neither of these papers address exactly the appropriate populations I<br>would argue that they are of relevance to the consultation and should at<br>least have been referred to.<br>I hope this is helpful|Comments noted. Please note that a table which<br>summarizes the Committee’s key conclusions<br>(including those related to health economics) is<br>included at the end of the document.<br>The Committee considered the results of the GO-<br>AFTER RCT in its deliberations. It considered<br>whether the results for golimumab from this study<br>could be applied to the other TNF inhibitors (see<br>FAD section 4.3.7).<br>A key uncertainty in the appraisal relates to the<br>efficacy of adalimumab, etanercept and infliximab<br>after the failure of a first TNF inhibitor. The ATTEST<br>study included patients with no history of treatment<br>with abatacept or TNF inhibitor therapy, and for<br>whom treatment with methotrexate had provided an<br>inadequate response. It therefore did not represent<br>the appropriate patient group for this appraisal.|

But until then we need to try the different agents to find one<br>that works for an individual patient.|The Committee recognised that not everyone will<br>respond to the same treatment (see FAD sections<br>4.3.2, 4.3.3) Although NICE accepts that individual<br>NHS users will expect to receive treatments to<br>which their conditions may respond, this does not<br>impose a requirement on the Committee to<br>recommend technologies that are not cost effective<br>enough to provide the best value to users of the<br>NHS as a whole (see ‘Social Value Judgement –<br>Principles for the development of NICE guidance;<br>principle 5)|

Some patients are likely to become severely ill without<br>these drugs, and may suffer disproportionate hardship, such as job<br>loss.<br>It is also surprising that we havent got more patient numbers in the<br>report (unless I have missed them). Cost effectiveness is different<br>from cost. Cost is a function of the cost of the drugs times the size<br>of the patient group. But there are no calculations that I can find in<br>this document, which assess this. Surely we need to look at both<br>cost effectiveness and cost? After all, if we are only talking about<br>100 patients a year, and these are patients with the most severe<br>disease, we may make a different decision than if we are talking<br>about 100,000 patients with moderate disease. So unless we see<br>these figures, its difficult to say whether this guidance is<br>reasonable.|Comment noted. Consideration of the severity of<br>disease and the prognosis could be considered in<br>the recommended research.<br>The Committee does not base its decision on the<br>potential budget impact of a technology. The<br>Committee takes account of how its advice may<br>enable the more efficient use of available<br>healthcare resources, as represented by estimates<br>of incremental cost effectiveness (see Guide to the<br>Methods of Technology Appraisal, section 6.2.14).|
